People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I know that sounds awful. I’m sorry. It just frightened me enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been longer than I’d like to admit. Not really… I mean, maybe once in a while there’s a small moment that’s okay, so I don’t want to overstate it.

I just… I don’t know, I hate even saying it out loud. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m… honestly a bit embarrassed to say it. I think I’m here because some thoughts have been scaring me lately. I know that sounds awful. I’m sorry. It just frightened me enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been longer than I’d like to admit. Maybe about a year and a half? Actually, closer to a year and seven months, I guess.\n\nIt started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load. I kept telling myself it was just stress and that I could handle it, so… I didn’t really say anything. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has changed. I mean, I still try to show up for things, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything completely, but it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even robotics club at school used to give me a little spark, you know? Lately I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel and tell myself I’m tired or busy, but if I’m being honest, it’s because none of it feels enjoyable the way it used to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your ability to experience pleasure from activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I still try to go sometimes, but it’s definitely not like before.\n\nI used to look forward to softball, book club, working in my garden… even robotics club with the students. Now I mostly just show up and go through the motions, or I cancel and say I’m too busy or tired. It just… doesn’t feel enjoyable anymore, I guess. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would carry out the act of not waking up or your family and students being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Um… I haven’t, like, made a plan or anything. I don’t want you to think I’m saying that.\n\nBut… yes, I’ve had thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. And sometimes, driving home, I catch myself wondering if my family or my students would be better off without me. That part scares me, because I don’t want to act on it. I just… I don’t know, I hate even saying it out loud. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, so I didn’t really think of it as a “weight change” at first.\n\nBut my clothes have gotten noticeably looser over the past year. And on school days I’ll often just… forget to eat, or kind of avoid it, until late afternoon. I know that probably isn’t great. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like that.\n\nMaybe I feel slowed down in my head sometimes, but I don’t know if I’m actually moving slower or restless in a way other people would see. No one’s mentioned it to me. I mean, I still technically do things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve just stopped everything.\n\nBut I used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even the robotics club at school used to feel fun and energizing. Lately I mostly just… show up because I’m supposed to, or I cancel and tell myself I’m just busy.\n\nIt’s not that the activities are bad. They just don’t feel enjoyable the way they used to. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, probably. I try not to dwell on it, because I know everyone has bad days.\n\nBut if a lesson doesn’t go well, or a student looks confused, I tend to take it really personally. I’ll keep replaying it afterward like it means I’m letting everyone down, like I’m just not good enough at this.\n\nAnd then it sort of spills over into home too, like I start thinking I’m a terrible teacher and probably not doing enough as a mother either. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m… honestly a bit embarrassed to say it. I think I’m here because some thoughts have been scaring me lately. I don’t mean that I’m going to do anything, I really don’t want to, but I’ve caught myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd sometimes when I’m driving home, I wonder if my family… or even my students… would be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I’m sorry. It just frightened me enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been longer than I’d like to admit. Maybe about a year and a half? Actually, closer to a year and seven months, I guess.\n\nIt started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load. I kept telling myself it was just stress and that I could handle it, so… I didn’t really say anything. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has changed. I mean, I still try to show up for things, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything completely, but it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even robotics club at school used to give me a little spark, you know? Lately I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel and tell myself I’m tired or busy, but if I’m being honest, it’s because none of it feels enjoyable the way it used to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your ability to experience pleasure from activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I still try to go sometimes, but it’s definitely not like before.\n\nI used to look forward to softball, book club, working in my garden… even robotics club with the students. Now I mostly just show up and go through the motions, or I cancel and say I’m too busy or tired. It just… doesn’t feel enjoyable anymore, I guess. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would carry out the act of not waking up or your family and students being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Um… I haven’t, like, made a plan or anything. I don’t want you to think I’m saying that.\n\nBut… yes, I’ve had thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. And sometimes, driving home, I catch myself wondering if my family or my students would be better off without me. That part scares me, because I don’t want to act on it. I just… I don’t know, I hate even saying it out loud. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, so I didn’t really think of it as a “weight change” at first.\n\nBut my clothes have gotten noticeably looser over the past year. And on school days I’ll often just… forget to eat, or kind of avoid it, until late afternoon. I know that probably isn’t great. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like that.\n\nMaybe I feel slowed down in my head sometimes, but I don’t know if I’m actually moving slower or restless in a way other people would see. No one’s mentioned it to me. Is that… is that helpful, or do you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nI do feel low a lot, but “consistently sad or empty most of the day” sounds so definite. Some parts of the day I can still function and talk to people, especially at work. But underneath it, I guess there is this… heaviness, or flatness, that’s there more than I’d like.\n\nDoes that make sense, or am I not answering it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel interested in activities, or has that changed for you over time?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, I think. I mean, I still technically do things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve just stopped everything.\n\nBut I used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even the robotics club at school used to feel fun and energizing. Lately I mostly just… show up because I’m supposed to, or I cancel and tell myself I’m just busy.\n\nIt’s not that the activities are bad. They just don’t feel enjoyable the way they used to. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, probably. I try not to dwell on it, because I know everyone has bad days.\n\nBut if a lesson doesn’t go well, or a student looks confused, I tend to take it really personally. I’ll keep replaying it afterward like it means I’m letting everyone down, like I’m just not good enough at this.\n\nAnd then it sort of spills over into home too, like I start thinking I’m a terrible teacher and probably not doing enough as a mother either. I know that may sound dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound like I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s definitely different.\n\nI used to really look forward to things like softball, book club, working in my garden, even robotics club with the students. Lately I either just go because I feel like I should, or I end up canceling. It’s more like going through the motions than actually enjoying it.\n\nIs that what you mean by losing interest?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I’d like to admit, honestly. I think around a year and a half, maybe a little more — about a year and seven months.\n\nIt seemed to start after I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load. At first I told myself it was just a busy term and I’d bounce back, but it’s kind of stayed. Does that timeline help?\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you're doing things that you used to enjoy, even if you don't look forward to them as much?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe once in a while there’s a small moment that’s okay, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut with things like softball, book club, my garden, even robotics club, I used to actually look forward to them and feel good doing them. Now I mostly just… show up because I feel like I should, or I cancel. When I do go, it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\nIs that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt this low?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there have been days where I manage better or I seem more like myself, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a real stretch where it lifted.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since around when I took on the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load… about a year and seven months ago, I think. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past year and a half where you felt completely back to normal for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but it’s really been going on pretty consistently since I took over that STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load — about a year and seven months now.\n\nThere have been better days, I guess, but not “back to normal” for months. Is that answering it clearly?\n\nDoctor: Has this low feeling you've described been present for most of every day, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: Not more than two years, no. It’s been… about a year and seven months, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign along with my regular teaching load.\n\nSo it’s been a long time, but not quite two years. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m… honestly a bit embarrassed to say it. I think I’m here because some thoughts have been scaring me lately. I don’t mean that I’m going to do anything, I really don’t want to, but I’ve caught myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd sometimes when I’m driving home, I wonder if my family… or even my students… would be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I’m sorry. It just frightened me enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been longer than I’d like to admit. Maybe about a year and a half? Actually, closer to a year and seven months, I guess.\n\nIt started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load. I kept telling myself it was just stress and that I could handle it, so… I didn’t really say anything. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has changed. I mean, I still try to show up for things, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything completely, but it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even robotics club at school used to give me a little spark, you know? Lately I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel and tell myself I’m tired or busy, but if I’m being honest, it’s because none of it feels enjoyable the way it used to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your ability to experience pleasure from activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I still try to go sometimes, but it’s definitely not like before.\n\nI used to look forward to softball, book club, working in my garden… even robotics club with the students. Now I mostly just show up and go through the motions, or I cancel and say I’m too busy or tired. It just… doesn’t feel enjoyable anymore, I guess. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would carry out the act of not waking up or your family and students being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Um… I haven’t, like, made a plan or anything. I don’t want you to think I’m saying that.\n\nBut… yes, I’ve had thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. And sometimes, driving home, I catch myself wondering if my family or my students would be better off without me. That part scares me, because I don’t want to act on it. I just… I don’t know, I hate even saying it out loud. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, so I didn’t really think of it as a “weight change” at first.\n\nBut my clothes have gotten noticeably looser over the past year. And on school days I’ll often just… forget to eat, or kind of avoid it, until late afternoon. I know that probably isn’t great. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like that.\n\nMaybe I feel slowed down in my head sometimes, but I don’t know if I’m actually moving slower or restless in a way other people would see. No one’s mentioned it to me. Is that… is that helpful, or do you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nI do feel low a lot, but “consistently sad or empty most of the day” sounds so definite. Some parts of the day I can still function and talk to people, especially at work. But underneath it, I guess there is this… heaviness, or flatness, that’s there more than I’d like.\n\nDoes that make sense, or am I not answering it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel interested in activities, or has that changed for you over time?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, I think. I mean, I still technically do things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve just stopped everything.\n\nBut I used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even the robotics club at school used to feel fun and energizing. Lately I mostly just… show up because I’m supposed to, or I cancel and tell myself I’m just busy.\n\nIt’s not that the activities are bad. They just don’t feel enjoyable the way they used to. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, probably. I try not to dwell on it, because I know everyone has bad days.\n\nBut if a lesson doesn’t go well, or a student looks confused, I tend to take it really personally. I’ll keep replaying it afterward like it means I’m letting everyone down, like I’m just not good enough at this.\n\nAnd then it sort of spills over into home too, like I start thinking I’m a terrible teacher and probably not doing enough as a mother either. I know that may sound dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound like I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s definitely different.\n\nI used to really look forward to things like softball, book club, working in my garden, even robotics club with the students. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m… honestly a bit embarrassed to say it. I think I’m here because some thoughts have been scaring me lately. I don’t mean that I’m going to do anything, I really don’t want to, but I’ve caught myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd sometimes when I’m driving home, I wonder if my family… or even my students… would be better off without me. I know that sounds awful. I’m sorry. It just frightened me enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been longer than I’d like to admit. Maybe about a year and a half? Actually, closer to a year and seven months, I guess.\n\nIt started around when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load. I kept telling myself it was just stress and that I could handle it, so… I didn’t really say anything. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has changed. I mean, I still try to show up for things, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything completely, but it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even robotics club at school used to give me a little spark, you know? Lately I mostly just… go through the motions. Or I cancel and tell myself I’m tired or busy, but if I’m being honest, it’s because none of it feels enjoyable the way it used to. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your ability to experience pleasure from activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I still try to go sometimes, but it’s definitely not like before.\n\nI used to look forward to softball, book club, working in my garden… even robotics club with the students. Now I mostly just show up and go through the motions, or I cancel and say I’m too busy or tired. It just… doesn’t feel enjoyable anymore, I guess. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would carry out the act of not waking up or your family and students being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Um… I haven’t, like, made a plan or anything. I don’t want you to think I’m saying that.\n\nBut… yes, I’ve had thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. And sometimes, driving home, I catch myself wondering if my family or my students would be better off without me. That part scares me, because I don’t want to act on it. I just… I don’t know, I hate even saying it out loud. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, so I didn’t really think of it as a “weight change” at first.\n\nBut my clothes have gotten noticeably looser over the past year. And on school days I’ll often just… forget to eat, or kind of avoid it, until late afternoon. I know that probably isn’t great. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like that.\n\nMaybe I feel slowed down in my head sometimes, but I don’t know if I’m actually moving slower or restless in a way other people would see. No one’s mentioned it to me. Is that… is that helpful, or do you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nI do feel low a lot, but “consistently sad or empty most of the day” sounds so definite. Some parts of the day I can still function and talk to people, especially at work. But underneath it, I guess there is this… heaviness, or flatness, that’s there more than I’d like.\n\nDoes that make sense, or am I not answering it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel interested in activities, or has that changed for you over time?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, I think. I mean, I still technically do things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve just stopped everything.\n\nBut I used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even the robotics club at school used to feel fun and energizing. Lately I mostly just… show up because I’m supposed to, or I cancel and tell myself I’m just busy.\n\nIt’s not that the activities are bad. They just don’t feel enjoyable the way they used to. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, probably. I try not to dwell on it, because I know everyone has bad days.\n\nBut if a lesson doesn’t go well, or a student looks confused, I tend to take it really personally. I’ll keep replaying it afterward like it means I’m letting everyone down, like I’m just not good enough at this.\n\nAnd then it sort of spills over into home too, like I start thinking I’m a terrible teacher and probably not doing enough as a mother either. I know that may sound dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound like I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s definitely different.\n\nI used to really look forward to things like softball, book club, working in my garden, even robotics club with the students. Lately I either just go because I feel like I should, or I end up canceling. It’s more like going through the motions than actually enjoying it.\n\nIs that what you mean by losing interest?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I’d like to admit, honestly. I think around a year and a half, maybe a little more — about a year and seven months.\n\nIt seemed to start after I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load. At first I told myself it was just a busy term and I’d bounce back, but it’s kind of stayed. Does that timeline help?\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you're doing things that you used to enjoy, even if you don't look forward to them as much?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe once in a while there’s a small moment that’s okay, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut with things like softball, book club, my garden, even robotics club, I used to actually look forward to them and feel good doing them. Now I mostly just… show up because I feel like I should, or I cancel. When I do go, it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\nIs that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt this low?\n\nPatient: Not really in any clear way, no. I mean, there have been days where I manage better or I seem more like myself, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a real stretch where it lifted.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since around when I took on the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load… about a year and seven months ago, I think. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past year and a half where you felt completely back to normal for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’ll keep replaying it afterward like it means I’m letting everyone down, like I’m just not good enough at this.\n\nAnd then it sort of spills over into home too, like I start thinking I’m a terrible teacher and probably not doing enough as a mother either. I know that may sound dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound like I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s definitely different.\n\nI used to really look forward to things like softball, book club, working in my garden, even robotics club with the students. Lately I either just go because I feel like I should, or I end up canceling. It’s more like going through the motions than actually enjoying it.\n\nIs that what you mean by losing interest?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I’d like to admit, honestly. I think around a year and a half, maybe a little more — about a year and seven months.\n\nIt seemed to start after I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load. At first I told myself it was just a busy term and I’d bounce back, but it’s kind of stayed. Does that timeline help?\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you're doing things that you used to enjoy, even if you don't look forward to them as much?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe once in a while there’s a small moment that’s okay, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut with things like softball, book club, my garden, even robotics club, I used to actually look forward to them and feel good doing them. I mean, there have been days where I manage better or I seem more like myself, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a real stretch where it lifted.\n\nIt’s been pretty steady since around when I took on the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load… about a year and seven months ago, I think. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past year and a half where you felt completely back to normal for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but it’s really been going on pretty consistently since I took over that STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load — about a year and seven months now.\n\nThere have been better days, I guess, but not “back to normal” for months. Is that answering it clearly?\n\nDoctor: Has this low feeling you've described been present for most of every day, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: Not more than two years, no. It’s been… about a year and seven months, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign along with my regular teaching load.\n\nSo it’s been a long time, but not quite two years. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is this low feeling something you experience for most of each day?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, most days it’s there for a lot of the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has this low feeling you've described been present every day since it started, or have there been days where you didn't feel it at all?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say absolutely every single day with no exception… there may have been a day here or there where it was lighter or I was distracted enough not to notice it as much.\n\nBut overall, it’s been going on pretty steadily since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load — about a year and seven months now. I just… I don’t know, I hate even saying it out loud. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, so I didn’t really think of it as a “weight change” at first.\n\nBut my clothes have gotten noticeably looser over the past year. And on school days I’ll often just… forget to eat, or kind of avoid it, until late afternoon. I know that probably isn’t great. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything obvious like that.\n\nMaybe I feel slowed down in my head sometimes, but I don’t know if I’m actually moving slower or restless in a way other people would see. No one’s mentioned it to me. Is that… is that helpful, or do you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling consistently sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nI do feel low a lot, but “consistently sad or empty most of the day” sounds so definite. Some parts of the day I can still function and talk to people, especially at work. But underneath it, I guess there is this… heaviness, or flatness, that’s there more than I’d like.\n\nDoes that make sense, or am I not answering it properly?\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel interested in activities, or has that changed for you over time?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, I think. I mean, I still technically do things, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve just stopped everything.\n\nBut I used to really look forward to softball, and book club, and working in my garden. Even the robotics club at school used to feel fun and energizing. Lately I mostly just… show up because I’m supposed to, or I cancel and tell myself I’m just busy.\n\nIt’s not that the activities are bad. They just don’t feel enjoyable the way they used to. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, probably. I try not to dwell on it, because I know everyone has bad days.\n\nBut if a lesson doesn’t go well, or a student looks confused, I tend to take it really personally. I’ll keep replaying it afterward like it means I’m letting everyone down, like I’m just not good enough at this.\n\nAnd then it sort of spills over into home too, like I start thinking I’m a terrible teacher and probably not doing enough as a mother either. I know that may sound dramatic, but it feels very real in the moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I don’t want to make it sound like I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s definitely different.\n\nI used to really look forward to things like softball, book club, working in my garden, even robotics club with the students. Lately I either just go because I feel like I should, or I end up canceling. It’s more like going through the motions than actually enjoying it.\n\nIs that what you mean by losing interest?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I’d like to admit, honestly. I think around a year and a half, maybe a little more — about a year and seven months.\n\nIt seemed to start after I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load. At first I told myself it was just a busy term and I’d bounce back, but it’s kind of stayed. Does that timeline help?\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you're doing things that you used to enjoy, even if you don't look forward to them as much?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe once in a while there’s a small moment that’s okay, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut with things like softball, book club, my garden, even robotics club, I used to actually look forward to them and feel good doing them. Now I mostly just… show up because I feel like I should, or I cancel. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past year and a half where you felt completely back to normal for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I wish I could say there was a couple-month stretch where I felt like myself again, but it’s really been going on pretty consistently since I took over that STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load — about a year and seven months now.\n\nThere have been better days, I guess, but not “back to normal” for months. Is that answering it clearly?\n\nDoctor: Has this low feeling you've described been present for most of every day, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: Not more than two years, no. It’s been… about a year and seven months, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign along with my regular teaching load.\n\nSo it’s been a long time, but not quite two years. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is this low feeling something you experience for most of each day?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, most days it’s there for a lot of the day. I don’t know if I’m judging it exactly right, because I can still get through things and talk to people, so maybe it doesn’t always look that way.\n\nBut underneath, it’s kind of there most of the day, yes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has this low feeling you've described been present every day since it started, or have there been days where you didn't feel it at all?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say absolutely every single day with no exception… there may have been a day here or there where it was lighter or I was distracted enough not to notice it as much.\n\nBut overall, it’s been going on pretty steadily since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my normal teaching load — about a year and seven months now. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has this low feeling you've described been present for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not quite two years. It’s been about a year and seven months, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load.
